On the basis of the pluralism and suspicion we have about how the Greek state has been structured in previous years, unfortunately, we have come to the conclusion that from one occasion we can lead to a general situation. The subject of the topical question concerns the effort to link women to clean-up, where they now face the range of redundancies, because they cannot join the single wage, despite the fact that their formal qualifications have been accepted at least until the 1980s for their recruitment in the public sector under contracts of indefinite duration. I would like to point out, Minister, that in one of the cases we are talking about there has been recourse to Greek justice. Already, a positive interim decision has been reached, according to which this person working for cleanliness in the Municipality of Naples - Thessaloniki has returned to work. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] I believe that in dentistry, with the goodwill you have already shown, and in oral communication so far, we will find a solution that does not produce any favor for those who will try to infiltrate into a transitional provision, but will also give justice to workers who are hard, paid for as far as possible, and who are also a key element in the functioning of the services they serve. I therefore believe that, on the basis of these facts, a solution can be found which concerns a wider set of working women. Thank you.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u3	Thank you too, Mr. Comrade. Minister, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u4	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Giannoulis, you raise a point which I personally did not know exists in these specific cases. I have to say that as soon as you have tabled the question, we have contacted both offices and we are trying to see exactly what is happening. This will continue in the next few days, in order to see exactly what has been done and what action needs to be taken to ensure that there is no shadow in the employment relationship of these officials. It is, in fact, a problem that was mainly with judicial decisions after 2009-2010, where the two works contracts were converted into contracts of indefinite duration. There was no stimulus there, that is, without being a graduate of compulsory education to make a project contract. But there was a problem there. This has been discussed, in particular at the time, by various directorates of human resources, mainly OTAs. Legislatively, in 2017, if I am not mistaken, with Article 52 of Law 4456, the issue has been resolved for those who have been converted into the conventions and who do not have the standard qualification. So we will see cases and in collaboration with the General Accounting Office of the State - because it is the wage issue, for which the General Accounting Office of the State is known - and we will see what needs to be done. What we have to say - and you are right - is that one of the greatest conquests of the post-doctoral age is nine-year compulsory education, which has drastically reduced the level of illiteracy in the country and I think that we should not show any tolerance in this. This does not mean that we should not see with sensitivity some cases, especially in elementary graduates, in the transition period in the early 1980s, but I think we can, in cooperation with the services too, find a solution so that there is no problem for these officials. Thank you.

We are now coming to the third with No 154/29-10-2019 a timely question for the first cycle of the MP of Heraklion of the Communist Party of Greece, Emmanuel Syntagaki, to the Minister of Labour and Social Affairs, on the subject: "Let the wages of the workers of "Creta Farms" be paid immediately and their insurance and employment rights ensured". Mr. Synthraki's question will be answered by the Deputy Minister for Labour and Social Affairs, Mr. Metarakis. Mr. Synatiakis, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u10	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, the 'Creta Farm' is in a state of bankruptcy - it is well known - despite the fact that in previous years it has emerged into a market leader, it has been projected for its innovation and its extroversion. Long-term behind-the-scenes negotiations, large-scale banks, aspiring investors, have been ahead of us in an attempt to save the business. They, in our view, are solely responsible for the current situation. Yet, over eight hundred workers and their families are dramatically experiencing job insecurity, risking losing their jobs, paying the price of choices and games, for which they do not have the slightest responsibility. Already, in various ways, staff have been reduced. On the altar of the company’s viability and competitiveness, major cuts have been imposed since 2012, in 2014, a adjustment of working time and other sacrifices has been made. The company owes the employees three to four wages, while the bank's liabilities are running, taxes, debts are still pending in municipalities and other outstandings. The Court of First Instance of Rethymno, as announced, accepted the request of the temporary administration to subject the ‘Creta Farms’ to Article 106A of the Banking Code. However, whatever solution is found, unfortunately the workers, on the basis of the legislative framework, will be the victims of this development, which fatally lead them to forced resignation. Minister, we ask you what you are going to do, firstly, to pay off the employees' prizes from the company, their allowances, before any investment scheme is selected, any solution. Secondly, in order to ensure that no job is lost, regardless of the investment scheme to be created, we ask that there be succession and that workers be also to the new employer, but also in the event of the company going bankrupt, workers should be given priority over the company's creditors. We would remind you that there has been a change in the bankruptcy procedure, with a purely class measure, which your own government had previously taken, namely the New Democracy, which, of course, was maintained by SYRIZA, where workers' rights were classified in the requirements of 25%, that is to say, a lower category. We therefore think that this should be addressed. It's unacceptable. It is a universal requirement to change this arrangement. I would like to say one last thing: we are asking you to freeze unfinished obligations for workers in banks, taxes and municipalities until they have been paid back and the situation has been normalised. Thank you, Mr President.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u11	The Foreign Minister for Labour and Social Affairs has the floor, Mr Metarakis.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u12	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Colleagues, thank you very much for this question about the Creta Farms company. The government has recognized the problems facing this company at the very first moment and has moved decisively in trying to find a viable and definitive solution, which, as you can see, prejudges the proper functioning of the company and the finding of new investors. Because without companies producing, it is impossible to keep these jobs in the long term. It is a debate that we had held last Thursday in other questions from the Communist Party of Greece. The Government has therefore taken an initiative immediately through the present Minister for Development and Investment, Mr Adonnis Georgiadis, to overcome the crisis and save the company. There were several meetings with the two shareholders, as, as you know, there were issues of understanding within the company’s shareholder composition and with the banks. On July 26th, an agreement was signed between the company, shareholders and representatives of the four creditors, with the aim of restoring normality in the operation of the company, finding a strategic investor, transferring trademarks, patent rights from Cyprus, in which they were found in Greece and research by sworn accountants. There was also exceptional financial aid, with increased safeguards and the consolidation of all the shares in the main shareholders. Already, temporary administration has been appointed, with a recent judicial decision, which seeks new interim financing from the banks, with a view to meeting its obligations, such as payroll and suppliers, equally necessary for the production of products and for the proper functioning of the company. As I said, the signals have already come to Greece, and that is necessary in order to find a new strategic investor. Six non-binding tenders have been submitted and we have a deadline on 18 November to conclude finding a viable solution. Of course, the Ministry of Labour continues to monitor the company's performance. I want to tell you that a new check was requested that went yesterday. From the Rethymnon RES they tell us that the audit was carried out normally, that the payroll was not paid, recorded and imposed, of course, and the necessary fines, as labour legislation provides. An issue has also been raised for a trialogue meeting between the employees of the company and the Ministry of Labour. It was not possible to do this until temporary administration was established. We have now moved on to the process and this tripartite meeting will be held in order to find the best solution for the protection of workers and the viability of the company, which is equally important.

Some received a half - a - and - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a - a wage, and a - a - for - a - for - a - for - a day wage. Who is responsible for this situation? Shouldn't the government take a stand on this? Let me say that the workers are not interested in the dispute between the Domazak brothers. The mismanagement, the bad business moves, the gambling on the stock market, the exposures of many millions of euros, but investment toys of entrepreneurs will not be paid by the workers themselves. These poor investment moves, the case with the company's patents, and the way the 'black' money is paid by Creta Farms to various offshore companies - these are heard - will not be paid by the workers, Minister. It turns out God finally is the profit at every price. And while all this is happening, the company was greatly financed by the banking system. It was the first company to join the famous Juncker package. However, despite the high profit rates and the continuous growth of its market shares, despite the sacrifices of the workers, it is now on the verge of bankruptcy and is unable to finance the production process as well. The raw reality, Minister, says that businessmen, whether gaining or failing, always have one thing in their minds: to maximise their profits, their personal benefits at the expense of workers and society as a whole. In our opinion, all of them - large shareholders, strategic investors, banks - are doing for the rescue of workers, but in practice they promote their own self-interests, always asking workers to make sacrifices and to pay for the broken ones. Workers have no reason to want the company to continue operating and to recover production. However, the cost of any rescue plan should be borne solely by the profits of the large shareholders, banks and investors. To enforce this, workers themselves must be fully aware of the real situation, the self-determination, the manipulation of these interests, the awareness of their own strength and, above all, the promotion of their own needs, the organization, and the assumption of their rights in their own hands. And on this road they will have the sincere and wholehearted support of the other workers and the class labour movement. Minister, it remains the question of safeguarding employees, regardless of any solution, of freezing their outstanding obligations on banks, taxes, municipalities, etc., and of safeguarding their working rights. This is what the workers are waiting for and for you as a government and as a ministry, since you are talking about the issues of sustainable development, etc., which, apparently, is not growth for many, is for the few, for the entrepreneurs. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u38	Thank you, Mr President. General Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, today's debate and vote in plenary on the draft law of the Ministry of Civil Protection entitled "Approximately International Protection and other provisions" is the result of a substantial thorough and useful dialogue within the Standing Committee on Public Administration, Public Order and Justice. At all stages of its processing in the committee, we had the opportunity to discuss the draft law, improving it and forming a modern and enforceable framework. The resulting text reflects the need to modernise and update Greek legislation on migration - refugee issues through targeted and innovative institutional interventions. Firstly, fragmentation of diffuse provisions and legislation is replaced by a clear, coherent and easily accessible legal framework. Secondly, the many unstructured Greek asylum system is corrected and gives its place to an improved and remodeled system. Thirdly, we are redesigning a system in line with the requirements of the Union legislator, which does not simply proclaim, but in practice respects the rights of asylum seekers. Fourthly, we are adopting clear and specific rules as to what applies to the Greek legal system and how it applies in the field, taking into account the need for asylum seekers to understand the procedure, under pressure of the difficult situation in which they come. Fifthly, we eliminate the consequences of a piece of legislation leading to a large number of applicants in a meteorological situation. The slow rates, complex procedures and inefficient management worked as a brake on the compelling of asylum seekers from this uncertainty situation.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u39	I'm sorry, Mr. Comrade. Please, ladies and gentlemen, do a little quiet so that you can follow the rapporteurs. How can you possibly contradict the arguments of the rapporteurs? Please, a little quiet! Keep going, Mr. Cappato.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u40	The interventions which I have mentioned enable public policy and the institutions of the Greek state in the field of immigration and refugee, in particular international protection, to as far as possible in the shortest possible time. As instruments for implementing these guidelines, the draft law includes the European Parliament Directives, existing national and European case-law, the rules of good legislation that clear and specific arrangements adopt, as well as the provision for continuous and at every stage informing applicants of their rights and obligations. In particular, the bill concerns the recognition and reception of third-country nationals or stateless persons as beneficiaries of international and subsidiary protection, common procedures for granting and withdrawing the status of international protection, as well as rules on the judicial protection of applicants. Having been mentioned in detail and in detail the contents of the draft legislation in the committee's meetings, it is useful to highlight the points that form the essence of our intervention. Article 24 describes the procedure for granting residence permits for beneficiaries of international protection and provides for the distinction of their duration on the basis of the type of status granted, refugee or subsidiary protection. In particular, there is a three-year duration for recognised refugees, while for beneficiaries of subsidiary protection a one-year initial licence with a two-year duration for each renewal. At this point it is worth stressing that in the identification process in accordance with Article 39, an assessment of whether the persons entering is vulnerable, while at the same time only those who are subject to the explicit registration of the categories of persons identified as vulnerable benefit from the specific reception conditions is carried out. Article 46 provides expressly that a third-country national or a stateless person seeking international protection shall not be detained for the sole reason that he has applied for international protection. The second paragraph of that article refers to detention on the basis of an individual assessment, for one of a number of reasons: first, to establish the identity, origin or nationality. Secondly, the risk of escape. Thirdly, since it poses a risk to national security or public order. Fourthly, there is a significant risk of escape. Fifth, the right to enter the ground. It is also important to provide for Article 47 with regard to the conditions of detention for applicants, namely the explicit provision of their separate detention by prisoners under the ordinary criminal law. In Article 50, we need to point out the provision for the submission of incoming third-country nationals or stateless persons in medical examinations to prevent the transmission of diseases. Article 58 describes the general principle for vulnerable persons, such as, for example, minors, disabled persons and the elderly, as well as the assessment of their specific reception needs. At this point it is worth pointing out the essential contribution made by representatives of operators in the debate in the committee, since in the context of legislative improvements, direct relatives of shipwreck victims, parents and sisters are added to vulnerable persons. In Article 68, entitled ‘Entry of Applicants - Exemptions’, it is appropriate to point out that applicants in accordance with paragraph 1 may remain in the country until the procedure for examining the application for international protection has been completed to the first degree and it shall be prohibited to remove them in any way. Article 70 requires the provision of an applicant’s card to be valid for a period of six months with the possibility of renewal until the examination procedure for the application for international protection has been completed. The right to access a lawyer or other consultant at his own expense is also provided for, and the way in which the applicants are to record the identity data and the possibility to modify them at all stages of the examination, but is not a reason to delay the procedure. The completion of the examination procedure in the first instance in accordance with Article 83 shall be mandatory to six months, with the possibility of extending three months in the event of mass arrivals by foreign or stateless persons. For the accelerated procedure, the examination shall be completed within 20 days, with the possibility of an extension of 10 days in accordance with the above mentioned case. A number of criteria are provided for this process in order to classify a country as a safe country of origin by drawing up corresponding lists. With regard to the secondary procedure, the decisions which may be relied upon in the appeal procedure before the Refugee Authority shall be laid down, the compulsory representation of the applicant or, together with the proxy lawyer, and the time limits for the decision to be taken in five categories, with a maximum time limit of three months. Finally, Article 86 sets out the cumulative criteria according to which a third country is considered safe and includes, inter alia, non-threatening life and freedom, the absence of a serious risk of physical harm, the risk of cruel and inhumane treatment, and the possibility of requesting refugee status. Ladies and gentlemen, the issue of the management of migration and refugee flows is the greatest management challenge in our country's modern history. Following the economic crisis, immigration has been the biggest problem in the past five years. As the Prime Minister, Mr Kyriakos Mitsotakis, has pointed out, over time the issue is becoming increasingly migrant. Management of migration flows will continue to be a major issue for many years around the planet. The institutional framework and legislation regulating policy in this field is a key tool for the successful management of the issue by Greece. The international protection bill of the Ministry of Civil Protection is a decisive interference in the legislative framework. The Greek state is in a legislative marathon, which must win, so that it does not remain behind international developments, so that it does not suffer the negative consequences of the phenomenon for the cohesion of our society, so that it does not have to follow what is agreed by other countries. This can only be achieved with a modern and enforceable legislative framework. The harmonisation and modernisation of Greek legislation in this matter is required to be permanent. This is, moreover, an international, acute and complex problem. Yesterday's meeting of the five largest countries of the European Union and the statements by the German Minister for Foreign Affairs on the forthcoming changes to the Dublin Regulation underline the need for constant vigilance in adapting us to international developments. In order for Greece to be at the negotiating table, it must first have its own credible national immigration policy. Today's bill is in the right direction. Ladies and gentlemen, we must not forget that as a basis for any debate we have on Greek society and the need to maintain its cohesion. Every legislative initiative that does not take into account the needs and problems of the majority of the Greek people is doomed to fail. The aim of hosting refugees who come to Greece in human conditions can only be served through an immigration policy that is accepted by society and is not considered an obstacle to the daily lives of people. There can be no rational management of immigration when it seems to the Greek citizen that the laws of the Greek state do not apply, that there are infringements without consequences. We need to hear people's cry of agony in the Eastern Aegean. We are talking about regions in the country with well-known characteristics in the field of national security. There cannot be, for example, without rules and without conditions for the free movement of people in our towns and villages. These are people who in many cases the Greek state does not even know who they are. It is not only the problem of any phenomena of invasiveness, but the self-evident condition for the Greek state to know what is happening on its territory, especially in remote areas. This is necessary for both Greek citizens and asylum seekers. With the lawlessness and chaos that existed since 2015, we have led local communities to despair. We were turning one group of immigrants against the other. We left unprotected children and women, north of criminal elements that act uncontrollably on hospitality structures. We were unwittingly helping us out illegal trafficker networks. This cannot go on. We are often accused of being opposed to reduced reflexes on humanity. If, ladies and gentlemen, what has happened over the last four and a half years in Greece had something to do with humanism, we probably lived in another country. The bill comes to cure a great weakness in the previous legislative framework, from which it became clear that the first victims were immigrants and refugees themselves and especially the weak. They were afflicted and lived in inhumane conditions. They were forced to wait for years to find out what their fate will be. They were victims of all kinds of crime and abuse networks. If we want to help them, they must enter under the protective umbrella of a legislative framework that ensures the application of the rule of law for all. Any refugee who really needs help, whoever really cares about the international protection process, whoever really belongs to vulnerable groups will benefit from the bill we are voting on today. However, any guest in our country will now have an unfavourable attitude. For example, if asylum seekers do not cooperate with the Greek authorities, the procedure for examining their request will be interrupted. The bill comes to correct the big mistake made in 2015 and cost Greek society dear. Greece since 2015, due to the mismanagement of the SYRIZA government, has become the easy target of the trafficker networks. The first step towards reducing migration flows is to stop Greece being considered the easy target, a field of enrichment of criminal trafficker networks. In order to achieve this, Greece should not be an attractive country for those operating the system and international law, without actually being entitled to international protection. Ladies and gentlemen, Greece, with this draft law, for the first time, acquires a single international protection law, incorporating all the directives establishing the common European asylum system that governs the management of the migration flows and the people we host in our country. The draft law we are discussing is necessary and timely, a draft law which is necessary because it faces a real problem, a draft law that is timely, because it takes account of the latest developments in this international phenomenon. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Just a moment, Mr President, please. But we must at the same time be realistic. The introduction of the law into Greek law will not solve, by magic, such a complex problem as migration. Nevertheless, it will be a great step forward so that Greece is no longer considered to be a country without immigration policy, a country which simply adapts to what is decided between Turkey and other European countries, without having its own policy. We must bring back the security and respect of the laws to Greek citizens and especially to the afflicted local communities. Greece generously provides the traditional hospitality of our people in conjunction with the absolute respect for international law for those who seek asylum in our country. As rapporteur for the bill, I invite you, ladies and gentlemen, to support the draft law of the Ministry of Civil Protection. It is an important intervention at the heart of the problem, which will help all Greek citizens, our state services, refugees and immigrants who are hosted in our country. It is a timely and necessary legislative initiative for Greece, a law of a country of modern, effective and ambitious. Thank you for your attention.

Also, when we find mayors toward us or groups of extreme right-wings, which even impose their will on the government and prevent the establishment of refugees, this is something that unfortunately needs to be restored. How will it be restored? It will be restored, Mr Ministers, by going to the Ministry's officials, as we did - because solidarity has prevailed in recent years - the representatives of the First Reception Service, the Secretary-General and the MPs, but also a movement of solidarity, in order to explain to local communities the necessity of this phenomenon, the urgency and the interaction, as was done in the majority of cases with local communities. This is a refugee policy. Otherwise, we will go to social automation and social cannibalism, which is not the beginning of the majority of Greek society and we must be clear. In no case is it codification because codification means that I am putting all the provisions we have had so far into the concept of reception. It means that I have put to asylum all the provisions that were in place so far, that I have put in the procedural guarantees all the provisions that were in place so far. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u58	Thank you, Mr President. I would remind the House that the consultation deadline given for such an important bill was short, only four days and even a weekend. Ladies and gentlemen, we are still at the beginning of this House's term of office, but I firmly believe that we are now inviting ourselves to discuss a bill which will ultimately prove to be one of the most important of the next four years, because we are facing a phenomenon that is not transient nor is it a matter of individual concern to our country, but has a global dimension. Mass movements of people from underdeveloped or underdeveloped countries to the developed countries are a phenomenon that has come to stay. We need to learn to live with this. This is an unavoidable turning point in the history of humanity, which the developed countries, of course, with them, are also called upon to manage with pragmatism away from ideological prejudices of any colour. What are the actual data that deletes the limits of legislative intervention today? The first is that the Greek society has not yet recovered from the shock of the mass arrival of foreigners manifested suddenly in the summer of 2015. Ladies and gentlemen, in 2015-2016, more than nine hundred thousands of people crossed Greece to other European countries. This initial shock, however, turned into chronic stress when public opinion realised that the state was unable to manage effectively the relatively few tens of thousands of people trapped in the country when the northern borders were closed down. Unfortunately, this inability to manage lasts until now. The second presumption is the undeterminedness of the problem. No one is able to formulate a reliable forecast of how the situation will be shaped even in the near future. First of all, why do these people come together, sometimes and surprisingly, from war zones and, secondly, why Turkey is managing the issue blackmail against us and in Europe, while at the same time increasing the pace of its migration and refugee flows are leading to our islands. The third presumption is that Europe has not yet decided how to deal with the issue. This is a time-consuming embarrassment, the cost of which Greece pays as the first host country. In other words, what one understands is that most European countries are willing to pay us out of hand, as long as we keep the problem away from their courtyards. Fourthly, there is a European but also global legal and cultural acquis that our country is allowed to circumvent only if it puts itself outside the civil society of the civilized nations. These are the basic principles of respect for and protection of human rights, as established by international law, the law of the European Union and the European Convention on Human Rights. The first three elements I mentioned, the management weakness of the administration, the undeterminedness of the phenomenon and the repatriations of the European Union have shaped a climate of distrust and fear in Greek society. This climate is constantly undermining the extreme right-wing demagogers in order to turn fear into anger, which they hope will be politically frustrated themselves, and that is why the fourth pretext of the principles and rules of international law, which is compulsory for our country, is on purpose. Because if you scratch the surface somehow, as genuine nationalists, of course, they are enemies of world civilization and anti-Europeans. How does the bill respond to the three elements that make the problem? I take the view of the European Union here, because the government is being called upon to give a political solution through the European institutions. First of all, it is clear that the effort made by the Ministry to balance the effective management of the problem with the requirements arising from the rule of law. However, this balance is achieved at the minimum allowed by the directives, and in some respects it is not even possible to do so. It is obvious in all parts of the bill that the legislator intends to dissuade, discourage arrivals, but also to strive with constant procedural details and impediments to applicants. There, however, where the objective of deterrent poses a direct problem of human rights violations is the field of administrative detention. There is even a three-year deprivation of freedom, without relying on a judicial decision, which is incompatible with the ECHR and the Greek Constitution. In the field of administrative management, however, the legislator, i.e. the government, takes a great risk. With a view to rationalising the process as a whole, the draft law makes detailed distinctions and classifications. I won't insist on details. Five individual host and identification stages, twelve case divisions based on priority examination and three additional reasons by priority examination shall be established. The border procedures provide for thirteen reasons for examination by the accelerated procedure and eight categories of cases, whereas in the applicant’s receipts, there are several different short and, above all, short deadlines for the validity of the cards, which will require lasting renewals, which will be an excessive burden on public services. We have the experience of the years of the 2000s. Temporary residence permits, then granted to applicants, needed lasting renewal and the services ‘trueed’. All this, ladies and gentlemen, requires constant coordination between the services, as well as a multi-human and very high level of administrative infrastructure, that is, things that are rare in our country. I wish the public administration could respond! However, a problem of administrative competence will also be created in the field of administrative detention as cases of detention can be spectacularly widened. In addition to the long-term detention, as I said before, there is a serious risk of overcrowding prisoners, with both the burden on the administrative structures of the country and the possibility that Greece will again be condemned by the Court of Strasbourg for inhuman and degrading treatment of detainees. Finally, there is also the undeterminedness to which I have referred, namely the uncertainty about the frequency and volume of mass arrivals. The only significant provision contained in the bill in the case of mass arrivals is recorded in Article 90, where guarantees are shrinking to an unbearable extent. These are, in particular, suffocating time limits within which asylum seekers exercise their rights. I fear, however, that even this welfare can prove totally inadequate in the event of extensive mass arrivals. In any case, however, the legislation will not be implemented unless it is accompanied by an operational plan, which, among other things, will provide for and organise the reaction of the Greek state to the three possible mass arrival scenarios. The nightmare scenario, first of all, of 2015-2016, the mid-term scenario of recent weeks and the third scenario, the most mild, the return to small and controlled flows of 2017 and 2018. In any case, however, it is necessary to ensure that local communities are co-operated in order to establish accommodation places for applicants for asylum or to establish detention facilities. It is not enough, Minister, to cooperate with the regional authorities. Well, of course you did. However, cooperation between the mayors must also be ensured. In the final analysis, only the mayor can convince his citizen, who pays a rent from his post, that it is not only fair but also practically beneficial to the entire Greek society, so that the asylum seeker may be housed free of charge. Here, of course, there must be generous compensatory benefits for the communes who work together, but possibly economic counteracts against those municipalities who refuse to fulfil their debt of solidarity, their debt of national marginality, which is borne by us all. It is not possible for the Greek state to insist strongly on the solidarity of European partners, when the country itself tolerates local rulers, who flatter the humbler instincts of their voters. As long as I mention a number. At the moment we are talking about the first administrative crisis, there are more than seventy thousand applications for international protection. After registration, where applicants finally remain in the system for examining asylum applications but do not comply with the transfer decisions, their examination shall be considered as a punishment within three days, because it is deemed to have been submitted to delay their removal. We do not exclude it from having such a case. And there are such cases. But that's not the rule. It is also foreseen that the breach of the terms and regulations of the host and identification centres will result in the cessation of housing and, in essence, in the removal of the applicants from them. Fourthly, with regard to access to education, there is compulsory monitoring of primary and secondary education, with a threat of sanctions being imposed on the family if it is not done. I mean, while it's a state case, you turn it into an individual. This is a challenge, when you have not ensured that children are transferred to schools. And we've asked a lot of questions about them. Teachers have not been appointed. There are no special educational tools for children who have another mother tongue. There are no interpreters, no reception classes in the morning schools. Fifthly, you are restricting the concept of family, effectively excluding the members of the family who are entitled to international protection from the regime in various ways. In fact, instead of maintaining the family, there is a breakdown of the applicant's family in practice, since by adulthood the child will now be considered as an adult, who can return to his country of origin, even if the rest of the family remains here. It's tough. At the same time, you are limiting the notion of vulnerability and, indeed, in the event of non-compliance with the transfer to a particular structure, there is an miscarriage from the asylum system. We consider it a dangerous article, because if we do not comply with the regulations on the operation of the structure, it lacks material living conditions, i.e. the elementary pile and roof even on stage. We ask you: With these people what are you going to do? Is he gonna be on the streets? Sixthly, you are putting strict procedures to put extra burdens and to fly out of the asylum system. For example, for simple matters, you are asking for the assistance of a lawyer, when the data themselves say that only 20% of asylum seekers received free legal assistance in the context of lawyers. So you're having a hard time. Even tougher is Article 77. We consider it unacceptable and ask for it to be withdrawn. Seventh: You are effectively canceling the right to appeal, since you are reducing the deadlines already too short. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u95	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, we are here today to discuss, following the preparation in the committee responsible, the international protection bill submitted by the Ministry of Civil Protection. This bill attempts to reincorporate into our own legislation three Community directives on the conditions and status of the recognition of citizens of third countries and stateless persons and applicants for international protection, but also to integrate all provisions relating to the asylum procedure. As MERA25, as we said at the outset, we consider that the excessively short consultation time has worked dissuasively for an in-depth discussion and analysis of the draft law. At least, during the meetings of the committee, we were able to listen to those involved in the management of the refugee organisations and indeed draw useful conclusions. We also consider that, instead of facilitating the situation, this bill does exactly the opposite, in practice putting innumerable bureaucratic obstacles, which eventually result in the violation of the rights of immigrants as described by Union law and international conventions. I shall mention, for example, a number of articles which we consider to be still, even after the legislative improvements made by the Minister, to be quite problematic. I begin with Article 20, where refugees suffering from post-traumatic stress are left outside the vulnerable groups, as it seems to us to forget that a large number of people arriving in our country come from war zones. Article 24 reduces the residence permit of the beneficiaries of subsidiary protection to one year, effectively abolishing the three-year Presidential Decree 141/2013, while there is no provision for those who have already received three years under previous legislation. At the same time, the same article considers that it is severely affected by the core of the preservation of family unity and respect for family life. Article 39, by means of its provisions, is even more burdened by the reception and identification service, without any provision of assistance, while at the same time providing for the detention by the Greek Police of those who are pending the appeal against a first-degree rejection decision. Paragraph 1 of Article 45 essentially reiterates the provision of 7 of Law 4540/2018, enabling a regulatory decision to be adopted regarding the imposition of a restriction on the freedom of asylum seekers in a specific geographical area. We believe that, although such a decision is lawful, in line with the provisions of the Constitution, it is essentially in line with the directives on reception, and completely disregards the conditions prevailing in our country, with the incomplete infrastructure and the full suppression of the services. The next article gives birth to and nourishes a climate of insecurity, undermining "the presumption in favour of freedom", making it possible to detain the applicant, despite having tabled a request while in freedom. At the same time, it degrades the Asylum Service to an agency that will simply be informed by the Greek Police. Article 48 provides for the detention of children and minors, which is completely opposed and violating the relevant international legislation, as is Article 37 of the Child Rights Convention. Article 53 introduces the restriction of access to the labour market for applicants since they can have access to the labour market after six months after the application for asylum has been lodged by holding the applicant until then hostage of any state-of-the-art benefits and easy victim of undeclared work. With Article 61, it continues to provide for the certification of victims of torture by public bodies, overlooking something we all know, ladies and gentlemen, that there are no trained doctors who can give such certification in our country, while the treatment of victims of such situations is limited to the provision of medical and psychological treatment, by neglecting or overlooking the need for these people to have complete rehabilitation and the complexity of these incidents. Article 62 raises practical obstacles, abolishing substantial guarantees for examining asylum applications. At the same time, there are many bureaucratic obstacles to the already extremely vulnerable and difficult position of refugees. Paragraph 14 of Article 65 enables an application to be made to be made to a representative of the structure in which a minor is hosted, without there being the assurance that he will always act on the basis of the benefit of the child, since he has no real or individual relationship with it in practice. A little further, Article 70 sets out in six months the duration of the applicant's card, without there being any guarantee that the renewal will take place in time and given the great workload of the services. Paragraph 5 of the same Article conflicts with Article 71(1), since the first paragraph provides for the removal of an application for international protection in the event of a rejection decision, and the second, the applicant's competence to a lawyer for whom a certificate of authenticity is required. How, however, will someone confirm the authenticity of his signature if he has been removed from the only official document that he has, the bulletin? In accordance with Article 77, a reasonable period shall be allowed to elapse, in order to prepare appropriately, by limiting the possibility for vulnerable groups, and personal interviews may, in exceptional circumstances, be carried out, as provided for by the legislative improvement, by personnel of the Hellenic Police or personnel of the Armed Forces, provided that such personnel have received prior basic training, in particular with regard to International Human Rights Law, the acquis of the Union on asylum and the techniques of the interview. We wonder, Minister, what exactly these exceptional circumstances consist of and, most importantly, what basic training will be given to these staff members of the ELAS. How will these positions be actually addressed? The presence of the applicant at all stages of the procedure, as provided for in Article 78, is of the opinion that it will eventually function inhibitionally, leading to a series of rejection decisions. Article 82(5) provides that decisions to a lawyer, the Head of the Regional Asylum Office, where the application was made, the Head of the Host or hosting centre, are to be performed in a fictitious manner. This provision on plastic performance also seems to be in place because it is likely that the refugees will not be informed in good time about the course of their application. Article 86 seeks to incorporate in its current S.H.E.’s decision. President. - The next item is the joint debate on the following motions for resolutions: At this point, ladies and gentlemen, you will allow me to tell you a little story, so that you can understand how safe Turkey is. In 2016, shortly after the implementation of the European Union-Turkey Agreement on the return of the ‘non-normal’ refugees entering Greece, Diem25, the pan-European movement of which is part of Greece, Mer25, launched a campaign with a unique demand to stop the implementation of this infamous agreement. That's why Sabir Ikbal, a 40-year-old immigrant from Pakistan stood. Sabir lived in peace with his family in a small town and had a small business. He lived quietly, until the day a local group of Islamic extremists attacked, in the area where he lived, a neighbor who was also a Christian asking for the confiscation of his home to become a religious school. Sabir, acting as every man should have acted, made a move: he helped his neighbor, which changed his life forever. He was designated "optional" because he helped another religion, so he had to leave his city. Members of his family and his close family were murdered by extremists, while he, on his journey to Greece, lost his father, who literally died in his hands, not withstanding the hardship and suffering of the journey. Sabir was left for a long time in Lesvos, while, despite the danger of his life, but also his already burdened health, he was found to be returning to Turkey, as it did, to find death, shortly thereafter, in a local prison. Such a country is judged to be safe, ladies and gentlemen, a country that treats refugees as criminals and imprisons them when it does not use them as tools to force the European Union and the West. Returning now to the draft legislation under discussion, Article 90 deletes all the exceptions of vulnerable persons and those who may be subject to the Dublin Regulation, by reunification, and we also have the exclusion from the planned special accelerated border procedure for people belonging to vulnerable groups. With paragraph 11 of Article 116, people belonging to vulnerable groups will enter the closed reception centres without any consideration of how they can live in them, given the circumstances, but also because they will be organized in the standards of pre-refugee centres, which are now under the Greek Police. Article 118 provides for the adoption of an established Healthcare Card from 2016 Otherlands, however, without any reference to the access to healthcare will be made to persons who have not yet received a card, persons who have been removed from AMKA, children for their vaccination, but also persons suffering from a serious illness and may need medical services immediately before they have obtained the applicant’s card. Several times, too, in the legislation under discussion, we have a reference to an expression which we have had enough, in a language that we assume they understand, in points of informing the refugees for procedures relating to their application or detention. So, what language will that be? How will it be ensured that these people understand sufficiently important processes for their future? As MERA25, we believe that refugee is a phenomenon that should be dealt with in a holistic and coordinated manner, with the coordinated efforts of all the Member States of Europe, by the creation of a European asylum office to examine applications and ensure the correctness of the procedures. The painful people who arrive in Greece do not come to Greece, come to European territory, and that is what we should have in mind when we legislate about them. As the manifesto of our mother movement, Diem25's aim is to create a "Open Europe activated by ideas and creative people around the world, which understands that fences and walls are weakness samples and trigger insecurity in the name of security". Let us not forget, ladies and gentlemen, that the Greeks are well aware of what is going to be a refugee and an uproar. We spent, among other things, the 1922 disaster, the migration of the '60s and the recent Brain drain of the memoranda. Before I close, you will allow me to make a brief reference to subparagraph (b) of paragraph 3 of Article 122 of the recently adopted development law, which incorporates into the eligible categories of projects for funding, from the national development programme, measures for the management of migratory flows, bringing us too many questions about what these actions may be. After all this, and as we consider that the draft law on 'International Protection' of the Ministry of Civil Protection, in practice creates more problems than solves, circumventing points of Union and international law, we can only vote against it in principle and on the whole. Thank you.

These statistics state unequivocally that the legal framework governing the provision of international protection does not work effectively. It does not provide protection, it accumulates applications, it stacks people both in the islands and in the hinterland. It does not discern immigrant and does not return the unrighteous, it testifies the strengths of the structures and local societies and to the Aegean and our islands and Continental Greece. This is a dead end. What is happening today is a dead end. And it's a passive policy. These uncertainties come to deal with the draft law that is currently being introduced before you. Firstly, its aim is to make this unstructured legal instrument of the five laws and a presidential decree, a single structured set of priorities, the examination that allows the administration to work, the judge to judge, on the ground that the applicant for asylum is at last aware of the course of his case. Secondly, to speed up the procedures so that in a maximum of 60 days the basic work of the Asylum Service will be completed and there will be a first decision, known to the applicant, on what is likely to happen with his application. Thirdly, to organise and speed up the returns of those who are refused, to prevent any paradoxical tactics to submit subsequent requests that delay in the relaunch and burden the administration. There are thousands of asylum seekers in the country who, for five or six years, have remained unfinished for exactly the reason I mentioned to you, namely, for the ever-new asylum applications, an ever-changing openness for them and for everyone. Fourthly, to prepare the matter from day one. And the question, ladies and gentlemen, is the integration, integration of these people. It is not just a legal procedure to provide asylum. It is a complex system and I agree with Mr Kamine who has previously raised the question of administration. It is a complex system of actions that the applicant has to know where he is, what will happen, what his rights and what his obligations are. So simple things. The new draft law calls for the asylum seeker to cooperate at all stages of the process. And to ask it, it gives him from the outset, from the very beginning, the possibilities and guarantees, i.e. information, legal protection, care and above all, result, that is, speed. So the purpose of this draft law is to speed up procedures, integrate people into society and ultimately returns. These three main objectives are being promoted to be implemented on a conditional basis - I agree, I repeat, with Mr Kamine - the issue of administration, namely that the public administration will respond, coordinate, produce results and ultimately achieve them. A pillar of the system is the consequence of the examination, i.e. the system for examining applications and the cooperation of the applicant. The plan is ambitious and bold. And I accept that as a competent Minister, because it is a bet for me personally, my colleague, Mr Kumucako, the public administration, the ministry, the government and the country, to be able to run it and implement it. In this direction we are working systematically to cover empty organic positions, to reorganise blind administrative systems, to ensure human working conditions for employees. So we hire four hundred seventy employees to the present Asylum Service, which has six hundred eighty people, to settle infrastructure issues in Athens - in KERANIS, which I told you - in Thessaloniki, in Rhodes and in Yannina, with new buildings to house the services. We reorganise the Service from incomprehensible and dysfunctional administrative patterns. Be careful, it takes six months to approve by the agency an expense, such as changing a car to Samos, there, to the CYPD. Every day, the asylum director needs to speak with thirty-five operational heads in the regional centres and the islands. It's unthinkable, it's revealing, but it's real, unfortunately. I would like to stand up and clarify two issues which have dealt with both the consultation and the debates and the hearings in the committee. First of all, I heard it again for the tenth time, that time was not enough. Why wasn't time enough? Time was sufficient. It's been six whole days. Six days were given to everyone. In fact, it was also heard that it was 'pretextual'. So I want to let you know that they were six days full. I would like to inform you that thirty-two comments have been submitted. So where's the pretext and where's the fast one? Where's that? Three hundred and thirty-two comments, half of which were accepted. Observations of both parties and, above all, of the Citizens' Counsellor almost entirely accepted. Fourteen comments were received from organisations and bodies, mainly on the conditions of detention procedures and unaccompanied minors. I therefore believe that it was a very successful consultation process. The time, ladies and gentlemen, is chasing us and chasing us, because boats in the Aegean Sea do not stop coming and as long as the days are over, because this is how the neighboring country has decided. This year we want to invest him usefully. The passionate chatter, the unstoppable disagreement is not our concern and, above all, it is not allowed. That's not allowed to happen to us. I would also like to make it clear that this is a draft law on the provision of international protection, asylum, as usual. It is not about immigration policy, that is. Because there are many issues, such as education, children in schools, a number of issues relating to integration, but which this bill does not deal with. You will allow me to conclude my report with two specific references. It is our belief that the immigration issue will continue for decades. It's a systemic, geopolitical and humanitarian dimension. He has been engaging in Greece in very difficult years, in the heart of the crisis. But, unfortunately, he was treated as something extraordinary, as a stranger. It's not like that. If we keep it up like the ostrich, let it go like an emergency that passes, then it's certain that the flow will pass above us, not next to us. Our political decision, therefore, is to tackle it systematically and seriously, to shield our borders as far as possible, to prepare the administration, to organise society, as a factor of resilience to society at every level. In order to do this, we need, among other things, to take it as a state, as a Greek Republic entirely, i.e. with a clear legal framework, adequate administration and long-term planning. The European Union has helped us financially and has also helped us by providing know-how, providing assistance with EASO. So are organisations such as the IMO, the International Organisation for Migration, such as the High Commissioner for Immigration. We cannot, however, continue to leave their management to third parties. Under this draft law, administrative care is clearly passed on to the Greek state, with an increase in the guarantees for the applicant, because the composition of the appeal committees is composed of three judges now. This provision, like the reintroduction of the annulling procedure in the Court of First Instance, shows the decision to incorporate all the right to asylum in a few years' time. The participation of a third judge from the present time helps to train a sufficient number of judges in this area of law. This is clearly a measure which is part of a short-term planning. The second point I want to make is that the law we are introducing today is Union law, it is European law. In other words, they are the directives of the European Union that we incorporate. What are we doing? We are faithfully transposing the European directive, without derogations and without broad interpretations. But we don't find new law to be clear. We are simply integrating European law, we are simply doing it properly - I repeat - and within the administration we are trying to be effective. The latter is an important one for the debate on rights that is taking place and it never ends this debate - and it does - but our principle is that every right granted correspondingly creates an obligation, a responsibility from the right holder. This is the central pattern, if you want, the core of this bill, namely the provision of rights and the responsibility that gives rise to this right. We do not reduce rights or guarantees. We provide everything that we can ensure and we ask the beneficiaries of rights and protection to comply legally with the rights granted to them. This is their responsibility to show them humanity, protection and to provide them with what they are entitled to under international law. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u207	Thank you, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, Minister, the government argues that the bill contains some important innovations. Of course, the margins of consultation have indeed been extremely suffocating and the provisions of the bill can undoubtedly lead to a de facto refusal to exercise the rights of applicants for international protection, on the practicable side, to exclude a particularly vulnerable category of people from asylum procedures, to deprive even of the right to effective and effective recourse. I move directly on to the articles because I don't have much time. Article 46 of the draft law repeals the automatic review of the administrative detention decisions provided for in Article 46(5) of L.4375/2016. Although there was a problematic implementation, the introduction of this characteristic was a result of recommendations from international organisations, such as the Special Rapporteur on the Rights of Immigrants. It is therefore a serious and unjustified regression for the protection of asylum seekers in Greece, while, with regard to this extension, the Government should turn to measures to ensure the effectiveness of reviewing detention decisions in practice. The extension of the maximum detention period, during which asylum seekers could be detained from forty-five days to fifty days, where the provision says ‘the reasons for imposing it continue’, is an exaggerated problematic provision for its misuse even in individual cases, which these reasons would not be met, so that this prolonged detention would run counter to the guarantees provided by Article 3 of the ECHR. Article 117(2) seeks the full establishment of the independent appeal committees by administrative judges, which has even deplored the Association of Administrative Judges, abolishing their participation in them by the High Authority of the United Nations, in accordance with the provisions of Article 5 of Law No.4375/16. During the current practice, these persons were almost all lawyers with exclusive qualifications and occupation in the Asylum Law and a significant international protection experience, as most of them have been serving asylum committees since 2013. These lawyers therefore come from a register of experts, which were included following a competitive procedure, which included a written examination and an interview. These committees, thanks, of course, to the valuable contribution they have made, so far have been fully successful, effectively, unbiased and with respect for the right to a proper prior hearing of the applicants for international protection, the administrative procedure on the requests in question. Moreover, the proposed regulatory change alters even the administrative nature of the committee, which is primarily responsible for formulating a substantive crisis, thus turning it into a quasi-judiciary, which is purely legal. This change, therefore, gives rise to questions even about the possibility of effective judicial protection in practice, since applications for annulment against the decisions of the committees will be heard by the administrative court, i.e. by lower-ranking judges in the judiciary, against the perpetrators, who will be members of the committees. It is generally possible to impose geographical restrictions in all regions of the country. We already know that the structures on the islands are inadequate, they are the most inappropriate, so even this generalization will create more deadlocks, not only in the islands but also in the inland. In combination with the previous measures, therefore, the position of the applicants is deteriorating and we consider it to create explosive situations. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Just a minute, Madam President. I do not have time to analyse all the articles I wanted. I just want to raise a crucial issue, which is directly linked to both the bill and the general policy on the refugee issue. We believe that the Dublin Regulation needs to be amended immediately. Union law is not simply not applicable. They're just as inadequate. I am referring in particular to Article 3 of the regulation, which considers that it is responsible for examining asylum claims it should be the primary state, the host State, without there being any predictions, practices, so that applicants in the host State are not, in fact, caught up in the host State, but that there is a level playing field, a proportionate burden-sharing in all Member States. We therefore believe that there should be a relocation mechanism between the countries of the European Union for their decent reception. And to this end we consider the recent statement by German Interior Minister Horst Zehoff, who, following a meeting with counterparts from the five largest countries of the European Union, talked about the need for a new philosophy in the management of the refugee problem. He pointed out that the Dublin Regulation has not been working for a long time, so it should be the basis for a future European Union asylum policy. We hope that this new German policy will be implemented. So the procedures must start from the host State, but must also be completed in the rest of the Member States. We consider this initiative to be important because a misunderstanding needs to be resolved: that Greece and any other host country are solely responsible and accountable for managing the refugee issue when the rest of the states so far simply wash their hands. So I will not agree that the European Union, as the Minister said, is helping us. The European Union despairs all its international contractual obligations, shifting the burden from economic and political to the states that welcome refugees. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u210	Thank you, Mr. Simopoulos. Mr. Tagaras from New Democracy has the floor for five minutes, and then Mrs. Gara from SYRIZA.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u211	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the refugee crisis today is an extremely important geopolitical, national, social and humanitarian issue. In order to deal effectively with the increasing migration flows to the Aegean islands, the closed borders and the lack of refugee reception infrastructure, we are bringing in a single legislative text all those arrangements that will be a useful tool for all those involved. With the bill we set clear and concrete rules, putting an end to the ambiguity of what actually applies at legislative level, which affected the operation of services, but also the applicants themselves, who were unable to understand exactly what the procedure, rights and obligations were. The largest part of the bill is that, for the first time, a law on international protection is laid down, including the directives establishing the common European asylum system, governing the reception, examination and recognition of applicants. This bill introduces important innovations. It includes rules which everyone should apply because otherwise they will be referred to the return procedure. This way we put a definitive end to anarchy and infraction at the reception stage. It is also provided for a right to remain in the country until the administrative examination of their application is completed in the first degree, as well as for when the applicants are not entitled to remain in the country even if they have appealed to the independent appeal committees. Thus, the main targeting of the bill, which is the return of the applicants already from the first degree, is being implemented. Rapid assistance from the asylum service shall be established, which shall, with the participation of the International Immigration Organisation, immediately after the registration of the application, inform the applicant of the progress of its case. The person concerned is thus facilitated to freely choose his voluntary return. Cases brought before the Court of First Instance shall be accelerated by shortening the time limits, both for the case and for the adoption of judgments. We are reducing the duration of the International Protection Bulletins. We are accompanying the duration of the cards with the adoption of the decision and not with its performance, as has been the case so far. We therefore oblig the applicants to contact the authorities more regularly and to make it easier to identify them. The bill we are called upon to vote today contains arrangements that make our country a sovereign state, which knows how to safeguard its borders, with respect always for human rights. The sense of security of the Greek society is strengthened and confidence in a rule of law is guaranteed. This bill is exactly what our country needs now, in order to manage this critical situation that our country is facing first and foremost and therefore Europe, by establishing an international protection regime for those who really need it. Ladies and gentlemen, it is our duty to respect human rights. But at the same time, all of us are responsible for the national security of our country. Thank you.

As Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis also pointed out, the refugee - immigration is a challenge on the basis of which solidarity and humanism are being tested and has managed to put the issue on the agenda at the last summit of the European Union. Ladies and gentlemen, this draft law brings together, until now, the relevant legislation, systemicises and adapts the relevant provisions in a single legislation in contrast to the current legislative framework, with which the phenomenon could not be dealt with effectively. It is also clear from the figures that sixty-seven thousand pending asylum applications, fourteen thousand outstanding refugees. And - if possible! - in the four years of governance of SYRIZA, just a thousand eight hundred sixty-six re-progressions. And yet the Qualified Opposition reacts to the bill. I sincerely wonder, ladies and gentlemen, from the Officerial Response: Are you satisfied with your actions to date, which have created images of shame for our country and, of course, do not reflect the humanism you call upon? Why can we forget, ladies and gentlemen of the Officerial Response, the devastating situation at the northern border, in Idomeni, in the terrible conditions for the people living in the camps, but also the consequences for the economy of the exclusion of the railway line, the image of Moria, the Aegean islands and mainland Greece? There is no doubt that all of this is not an honor to a modern European state! This situation, ladies and gentlemen, cannot be perpetuated by burdening our country and its international image. A stricter but even more just system for granting asylum is therefore introduced by this bill, and only to those who deserve it, while respecting the human rights of the applicants, but without any margin of abuse. So the main aim of the bill, which is clearly required, is to speed up the process of controlling asylum applications and the return procedure for those who are not entitled to asylum. Finally, the appeal-making machine, where the Asylum Service has so far been sharing with the rejection decision and pre-printed documents with the appeal, ceases. By the provisions we are discussing today, the application now presupposes a specific document on the basis of a similar application of the administrative procedure applicable to all cases, whether foreigners or Greeks. It also ceases the phenomenon of thousands of unpaid decisions on applications because it is not possible to identify the applicants. Proceedings can now also be done to a prosecuting lawyer or to an adversary, which is absolutely a legitimate process throughout our jurisprudence. Here I would also like to point out that a number of objections have been raised, particularly from the Qualified Opposition, to points in the bill, such as, for example, the applicant's self-determination. However, it is necessary, ladies and gentlemen, to ensure that each applicant is legally identified separately, as is the case for the individual inspection of applications, it is essential to examine in detail, objectively and non-discriminatoryly on the basis of individual examinations. Because the identity of the person asking for asylum from our country, as well as the objections to the composition of the appeal committee, must be investigated. Here you will allow me to say that the formation provided for by three administrative judges provides all the guarantees for the proper, objective and rapid examination of the appeals, taking advantage of the important legal knowledge of the members of the committee. Ladies and gentlemen, the SYRIZA Government has proved to be unable to manage effectively the refugee - immigration problem, on the one hand, because it has sent out the wrong message of attractive destination, on the other hand, because it has proved inadequate to establish a proper procedure for identifying and granting asylum to those entering our country. Clearly, the approach must be based on respect for human rights in the interpretation of the Court of Human Rights and Community law. And that is precisely the reason why there is a distinction between those in need of international protection and those who do not fulfil the conditions. We must therefore manage, for the above reasons, as a responsible state, the conceived and uncontrolled establishment of all those who enter our country by laying down specific rules, because that is how every favoured state must operate. And in this very right direction this bill is moving. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-31-S1-commons.u297	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, with this draft law, national legislation is in line with Union law and the directives set up by the common European asylum system, while in a balanced and proportionate manner it defines international protection policies, taking into account the increased flow of movement and the capacity of our country for the reception and integration of third-country nationals and stateless persons. We must also point out, because more than one side has been criticised, that the application of Union law is not a card. We do not choose every time whether to apply it or not. The criticism made when Union law is not applied or when it is delayed, is justified. The opposite is unjustified and ignores a basic principle that Europe is a web of rights and obligations. The fundamental principles adopted by this bill are self-evident in the civilised world. Greece is a country with land and sea borders, which has an obligation to protect and safeguard, that it is also a favoured state entity that does not allow those who have no legitimate cause to stay on its territory, and above all that the effective protection of those who are truly entitled to asylum presupposes the existence of a firm and effective rule of law. In short, it is a complex, strict but just legislative framework, which provides for flexible and rapid procedures with inappropriate timetables. At the same time it respects democratic legitimacy and international human rights conditions from the first to the last stage. Today, during the debate in plenary, this draft law has been criticised with arguments of diameter rather than completely different ideological origins. They have heard about intolerance or crimes against the nation, on the one hand, that it is a tough, unscrupulous and restrictive law plan and, on the other, that it is a European inspiration legislation that adopts too liberal concepts. In this sense, one might argue that it is a prudent and moderate piece of legislation, but bold and ambitious, which politically reflects the middle space. However, with the dimensions that the phenomenon has taken place in today, with the asymmetric flows of refugees and migrants, it is clear that ideological searches are excessive luxury. Common sense, in terms of consensus, is needed to tackle a top national issue urgently. Ladies and gentlemen, it is well established that a serious deficit has been presented in the past at a management and organisational level to deal with the refugee - immigration. There was a management slackness, which combined with the pathogens of the Greek administration and the ambiguities of the law allowed the abuse of asylum law to a large extent. This pressure was not too late to come by working on the migration from the neighbor's leadership. Of course, before we got here, we had both the time and the resources that went beyond EUR 2.2 billion, in order to structure a more reliable and resilient mechanism for granting asylum, refunds and integration. Unfortunately, resources have been dispersed and our country has remained undisturbed. However, it was not only the Greek society that tested the consequences of this mixture of administrative insufficiency and ideology. There have also been corresponding objections internationally by human rights defenders. Since September 2018, the International Committee on Legal Affairs and the European Council on Refugees denounced the systemic imperfections to Greek law, and the practice resulting from unaccompanied children in Greece to be deprived of the right to housing, health and education. In fact, the Committee on Social Rights accepted the complaint, as were similar complaints in the past and the Secretary-General of Amnesty International. There is no doubt that the broad interpretation of vulnerability and the ideological and inappropriate identification of the concept of immigrant and refugee violated the Geneva Authority and the rights of refugees, created stores of souls, vagaries and "Mories". To conclude and not abuse the time, I would like to say that in a world of closed borders, where their dreams of young people and hopes for a better life are dying in frozen containers, our small and poor country has lifted a burden greater than its own. I wish it could cope with and alleviate the injustice and misery involved in the distribution of wealth on three continents. However, what he can certainly do is develop social inclusion policies for immaculate people, demonstrating humanity, solidarity and understanding, these universal virtues that come from ancient Greek society, which gave birth to the sacred institution of question for desperate people who seek refuge to protect themselves from war and violence. Thank you very much.
